Added SS3711: when an arg's declared type and the callee input's type resolve to different base types (and neither is a typeParam), reject — the language has no implicit coercion. Validated: String->Int64 rejects, matching types accepted, full 1122-test suite + run_examples (472 assertions, 226 fmt-idempotent) ALL GREEN so no valid program regressed. Test: test_user_op_arg_type_mismatch_rejected. REMAINING (the larger 'one signature table' ask): dotted/external `.semsig` targets are still skipped (`if "." in target: continue`) — arity/slot/type/result/catch-type/fallibility/cleanup checking against std .semsig + imported-module docs, plus context/opaque-arg suppression and table-driven completions, are not yet built. Those need a unified external-signature table and are deferred as a dedicated piece.)_
- [ ] R-074 **Dropped-result and discard analysis must consult external signatures.** Evidence: `_target_is_nonvoid` recognizes selected builtins and user ops but not value-returning `.semsig`/external targets, so ignored results can evade the `out`/`ignore` discipline. Impact: resource handles, errors, allocations, and response bodies from externals can be silently dropped even though the source language is supposed to make effects/failures explicit. Fix direction: extend signature metadata with return kind, ownership, fallibility, and required disposition; make every call require `out`, `catch`, `ignore`, or cleanup/discard rows according to that signature; unknown external targets should fail closed unless explicitly declared `Void` and infallible. Agent guard: repair plans must add explicit `ignore` rows only when docs say discard is safe, otherwise add handling/cleanup skeletons. ->test: `.semsig` function returning `String`/handle without `out` rejects; void external accepts no `out`; fallible external requires `catch`; owned return requires cleanup or transfer; unknown external missing signature rejects. _(attempted 2026: extending _target_is_nonvoid to flag dropped family-intrinsic results (sqlite/json/bcrypt/log via _FAMILY_RT retkind) correctly catches leaked handles/ignored statuses, BUT it fires during parse and conflicts with (a) cleanup-worker / negative-test fixtures that intentionally omit catch on a close/finalize, and (b) minimal parse fixtures (a bare call with no out). Closing it safely needs a cleanup-worker exemption + corpus-wide fixture disposition updates — a deliberate design, not a one-shot change. Reverted to keep the suite green; left open.)_
- [ ] R-075 **Record equality must be semantic, deep, and type-directed.** Evidence: `_emit_compare` compares record fields directly and uses integer-style comparison for non-floats; `String` fields become pointer equality and nested records are not recursively deep-compared. Impact: equality can report false negatives/positives based on address identity, making guards, tests, caches, and security comparisons unreliable. Fix direction: generate type-directed equality: numeric/Bool by value, Float with documented NaN semantics, String by bytewise UTF-8 content, nested records recursively, enums/errors by discriminant+payload, and opaque/handle types only if an equality contract exists; steer secret strings to constant-time compare. ->test: two distinct equal strings inside records compare equal; nested record equality works; opaque handle equality without a contract rejects; secret record field equality uses or requires constant-time policy. _(partial: the core correctness bug is fixed — record equality is now deep and type-directed. _emit_compare's record path delegates to a new recursive _value_eq/_record_field_eq: a String field is compared bytewise via strcmp (NOT the pointer identity an integer compare gave — the old code did `icmp_signed` on every non-float field, so a String field compared addresses and a nested-record field would even mis-lower), a nested record field recurses fieldwise, Float by value (ordered), and numeric/Bool/enum-discriminant by value icmp. Validated: IR shows strcmp for String fields + icmp eq i64 for Int64 + no `icmp eq i8*`; a JIT run proves two records whose String field comes from distinct allocations (a literal vs a string.concat) with equal content now compare EQUAL (test.assertTrue PASS) — a false negative before. Full 1126-test suite green; the existing Point(Int64) equality test still passes. Test: test_record_equality_is_deep_and_type_directed. REMAINING: two listed sub-points — opaque/handle-typed field equality should REJECT unless an equality contract exists (currently falls through to a value icmp), and a secret-typed String field should use/require a constant-time compare (currently plain strcmp); both are security-policy additions on top of the now-correct structural equality.)_
- [ ] R-076 **Numeric conversions need checked/default-safe semantics before unchecked casts are agent-suggestible.** Evidence: WS1-095/WS3-014 are checked while `_emit_convert` lowers narrowing with `trunc` and float->int with `fptosi`, with trap/result/wrapping/saturating variants still pending. Impact: agents can generate `convert.*` rows that silently truncate, overflow, or invoke undefined/target-dependent behavior, which conflicts with the no-undefined-execution safety goal. Fix direction: make default narrowing and float->int conversions checked (`Result`/trap with SSR code), add explicit `convert.wrapping*` and `convert.saturating*` spellings, define NaN/infinity/out-of-range behavior, and require agents/docs to prefer checked conversions. ->test: Int64->Int32 overflow returns/traps with ConversionError; NaN/inf float->int rejects predictably; wrapping/saturating variants produce documented results; repair hints suggest checked conversion plus error handling. _(partial: BOTH checked-by-default conversions now land. (1) int→int narrowing traps SSR0012 (prior work). (2) NEW: float→int is checked — _emit_convert guards `fptosi` (which is UNDEFINED for NaN/+/-inf or an out-of-range value) with an ordered range test against the destination's [min, max+1] as exact powers of two: an ordered fcmp is false when either operand is NaN, so NaN/inf and out-of-range all trap via ss_panic SSR0014 (new runtime diagnostic), while an in-range value still truncates toward zero (the documented behavior). JIT-validated: 9999999999.0->Int32 traps (rc 134, SSR0014); 5.7 and -3.9 convert cleanly; the existing 3.9->Int64==3 truncation + all convert/example tests stay green; full 1127-test suite passes. Test: test_float_to_int_conversion_is_range_checked. STILL PENDING: the explicit `convert.wrapping*` / `convert.saturating*` opt-out spellings (a feature addition for callers who WANT modular/clamped semantics) — the default is now safe, so unchecked casts are no longer silently agent-suggestible.)_
- [ ] R-077 **Untrusted decode limits must cover depth, element count, unknown fields, and runtime enforcement.** Evidence: X-077 is checked, but `_validate_decode_limits` only requires `limit maximumBytes`; max depth, max element count, unknown-field policy, and over-limit runtime `DecodeError` are deferred. Impact: a decoder can satisfy the current lint while still being exposed to deeply nested or high-cardinality input and type-confusion via unknown fields. Fix direction: require `limit maximumBytes`, `limit maxDepth`, `limit maxElements`, and an explicit `unknownFieldPolicy reject|capture|ignoreBecause <reason>` for rawExternal decoders; lower/document runtime checks in `standard.json`/codec arenas. Agent guard: scaffolds for JSON/HTTP handlers must emit all required limit rows with conservative defaults, not just bytes. ->test: rawExternal decode missing each individual limit rejects; unknown fields reject by default; over-depth/over-elements runtime returns `DecodeError`; generated web scaffold includes all decode limits. _(partial: the SOURCE-LANE completeness gap is closed. _validate_decode_limits previously required only `limit maximumBytes`; it now requires the FULL bound set on any rawExternal decode (json.parse/decode/createDocument/codec.decode) — `limit maximumBytes` (size), `limit maxDepth` (nesting, vs a deep-nest stack blowup), `limit maxElements` (cardinality, vs a huge flat array/object), AND an explicit `unknownFieldPolicy reject|capture|ignoreBecause <reason>` (vs type-confusion via unmodeled fields; ignoreBecause requires a justifying reason). Each missing bound/policy is SS3077. Added unknownFieldPolicy to the call/task predicate set. Validated: complete decode accepts; each of the 4 dropped rows rejects; ignoreBecause-without-reason rejects, with-reason accepts; full 1131-test suite green (no apps/examples use rawExternal decode, so nothing regressed). Tests: test_untrusted_decode_requires_every_bound + updated accepted test. Closes the ->test clauses 'rawExternal decode missing each individual limit rejects' and 'unknown fields reject by default'. REMAINING (runtime + scaffold): the json/codec RUNTIME must actually ENFORCE maxDepth/maxElements during parse and return a DecodeError on over-limit input (a C-runtime change to the json parser — currently json execution is deferred), and `semanticscript new`/scaffold must EMIT all required limit rows with conservative defaults.)_
- [ ] R-078 **SSRF protection must be capability-backed allowlist enforcement, not only static private-host filters.** Evidence: completed X-075 currently rejects obvious literal localhost/private/cloud-metadata URLs, but real host/scheme allowlists, dynamic URL checks, encoded/obfuscated hosts, IPv6 private ranges, DNS rebinding, userinfo tricks, redirects, and runtime guard behavior are not enforced. Impact: agents can pass a `HttpSafeUrl`-looking value through dynamic construction or encoding tricks and bypass the intended network authority boundary. Fix direction: represent network authority as `capability <name> net.<scheme>.<host-or-pattern> connect`; validate static literals against the allowlist; lower dynamic requests through a runtime URL parser/normalizer that rejects private ranges, forbidden schemes, redirects to forbidden hosts, and DNS rebinding according to policy. Agent guard: generated clients must request the narrowest host/scheme capability and must not suggest broad `net.*` grants without `because`. ->test: encoded localhost, IPv6 loopback/private, integer/octal IP, userinfo host confusion, redirect-to-private, and dynamic URL fixtures reject; exact allowlisted HTTPS host accepts; broad allowlist requires visible rationale. _(partial: hardened the STATIC-literal SSRF check (SS3075) to defeat the common host-obfuscation tricks the marker list missed — _url_host now strips the `user@` userinfo (so http://evil.com@127.0.0.1/ resolves to the real host after the last @), the :port, and percent-encoding (%6c%6f… -> localhost), and _is_internal_host decodes integer/hex/octal IPv4 (2130706433, 0x7f000001, 0177.0.0.1) and IPv6 literals and classifies them via the stdlib `ipaddress` module (is_private covers 10/8·172.16/12·192.168/16 + IPv6 fc00::/7; is_loopback 127/8·::1; is_link_local 169.254/16·fe80::/10; plus reserved/unspecified/multicast). Validated: integer/hex/octal IP, IPv6 loopback/unique-local/link-local, userinfo confusion, percent-encoded localhost, loopback:port, and cloud-metadata all reject SS3075, while name/public-IP/port externals (api.example.com, 8.8.8.8, example.org:8080) still ACCEPT (no false positives); full 1129-test suite green. Test: test_ssrf_obfuscated_internal_hosts_rejected. Closes the encoded-localhost / IPv6 / integer-octal-IP / userinfo-confusion ->test clauses. REMAINING (the capability-backed + runtime parts): the `capability <name> net.<scheme>.<host-or-pattern> connect` allowlist grammar + 'broad allowlist requires visible rationale', dynamic (non-literal) URL checks through a runtime URL parser/normalizer, redirect-to-private rejection, and DNS-rebinding — a design change spanning new capability grammar + a runtime URL guard.)_

- [ ] R-080 **Island indentation and typed-comment handling need fail-closed parser behavior.** Evidence: WS1-012/X-067 claim mixed-indent rejection, but parsing strips a common positive-space prefix and only rejects tabs; typed-comment tokens are recognized but not retained as semantic metadata. Impact: agents can paste visually ambiguous islands or important `# security:`/`# failure:` notes that disappear from downstream docs/lints, weakening reviewability. - [ ] R-083 **Completed-security todo closure must require negative sibling tests and agent-surface fixtures.** Evidence: multiple checked security/correctness todos implemented the direct happy-path or obvious rejection but left adjacent bypasses: trust laundering, context-specific sink confusion, non-console secret leaks, shallow SSRF, partial decode/DoS bounds, and stub supply-chain digests. Impact: the checklist can read safe while agents still have easy leaky paths to generate. Fix direction: add a closure gate for any security/correctness todo: at least one direct positive test, one direct negative test, one transitive/wrapper negative test, one external/signature negative test where applicable, and one agent-surface fixture (`docs get`, `scaffold`, `fix --plan`, or completion) proving the tool steers toward the safe pattern. Agent guard: CI should fail if a completed X6/X7 item lacks sibling-bypass tests or if docs/examples show the unsafe shorthand. ->test: mark a security todo complete only when its closure manifest names these fixtures; a deliberate unsafe scaffold/doc example fails the guard; the security matrix distinguishes "direct-only" from "transitive-safe" enforcement.
- [ ] R-084 **Async/task guard semantics must become real state semantics before agents can rely on them.** Evidence: WS1-065/WS1-107/WS3-003 are checked with the single-thread backend lowering `start` eagerly, `join`/`poll`/`cancel`/`detach` as no-ops, `ifReady` as always-taken, and `ifPending`/`ifCanceled` as fall-through. Impact: source that appears to handle readiness, pending work, cancellation, timeout, and races is only documenting those paths today; agents can generate unreachable cancellation/pending branches and believe work is bounded or cancelable when it is not. Fix direction: define a task state object in the runtime, lower `start`/`poll`/`join`/`cancel`/`detach` to state transitions, make `ifReady`/`ifPending`/`ifCanceled` inspect that state, connect timeout/budget/cancel tokens to structured errors, and keep the single-thread backend explicit as a deterministic scheduler rather than a semantic shortcut. Agent guard: docs, scaffolds, and fix plans must mark async examples as single-thread fallback unless the runtime state model is present, and must not claim cancellation or pending branches are executable on the fallback. ->test: a task that is not complete takes `ifPending`; a canceled task takes `ifCanceled`; `ifReady` is false before completion; timeout/cancel produces the documented error; single-thread fallback examples expose their deterministic scheduling mode in JSON/docs.
- [x] R-085 **`lint --json` is not a versioned `sem.*.v1` envelope and breaks on parse errors, unlike `check --json`.** Evidence: `python semanticscript.py lint --json examples/hello_world.sem` prints a bare `[]` (the legacy raw `diagnostics_json` array), and `lint --json` on a parse-error file prints a **plaintext** `semanticscript: line N: …` message (not JSON) — whereas `check --json` on the same two inputs emits a proper `{"surface":"sem.check.v1", …}` envelope. README link: `§32.3 #17` / WS4-111 require every `--json` surface to be a versioned `sem.*.v1` envelope, and the MCP server (WS4-110) + `nextCommands` consumers parse these. Impact: an agent/MCP client calling `lint --json` gets a non-envelope on success and **non-JSON on a parse error**, so it cannot parse the result or distinguish `ok`/`ok-with-warnings`/`lint-diagnostics`/`compiler-error`/`tool-error`; the inconsistency between `lint` and `check` also means the same diagnostics arrive in two shapes. Fix direction: route `lint` (and every `--json` subcommand) through one envelope builder — success → a `sem.*.v1` `{surface, ok, status, diagnostics, nextCommands}` object; parse/IO failure → a `compiler-error`/`tool-error` envelope (never plaintext, never a bare array). Then audit all `--json` commands for the same gap. ->test: every `--json` subcommand emits valid JSON carrying its `surface` id on (a) a clean file, (b) a lint-diagnostic file, and (c) a parse-error file; a parse error yields `status: compiler-error` (not plaintext); the MCP `tools/call lint` round-trips the envelope. §32.3 #17/WS4-111 _(done: cmd_lint now emits a versioned sem.lint.v1 envelope with structured diagnostics; a parse error yields status:compiler-error JSON, never plaintext. Verified on clean/warning/parse-error inputs.)_
- [x] R-086 **`sem fmt` strips trailing comments, violating §2 ("the formatter preserves trailing comments in place; `sem fmt` does not strip them").** Evidence: a file containing `main return ok   # this trailing comment must survive fmt` run through `python semanticscript.py fmt` produces output with the comment **removed** (grep for the comment text in the formatted output fails; fmt exits 0). README link: §2 "Comments" — trailing comments are preserved by `sem fmt`. Impact: formatting silently destroys author rationale captured in trailing comments **and** typed comments (`# rationale:`/`# security:`/`# todo:`), so canonicalization is **lossy** — every `fmt` loses context, and the X-014 "docs generated from typed comments" path breaks. Fix direction: the lexer/parser must attach a trailing comment to its row (and retain full-line comments at their position) and the formatter must re-emit them; the round-trip must be comment-preserving and idempotent. ->test: a row with a trailing `# ...` and a full-line `# rationale:` survive `fmt` with their text intact; `fmt` is comment-preserving and idempotent over a comment-bearing corpus. §2/§22 _(done: Row gained a `comment` field and Entity gained `lead`/`comment`/`trailing`; parse attaches trailing comments in place and accumulates full-line comments into the entity header block; format_entity/_emit_rows re-emit them. Also forced UTF-8 stdout/stderr so em-dash/Unicode comments no longer crash fmt on Windows cp1252. Verified: trailing + full-line comments survive, and fmt is idempotent + comment-preserving over all 168 examples.)_
- [x] R-087 **`graph`, `query` (and likely other subcommands) reject `--json` with an argparse usage error, despite §24 "All commands accept `--json`".** Evidence: `python semanticscript.py graph examples/hello_world.sem --json` and `python semanticscript.py query examples/hello_world.sem kind --json` both print `usage: semanticscript [-h] {lex,parse,...}` (argparse "unrecognized arguments: --json", exit 2), whereas `slice ... --json` and `check ... --json` accept it. README link: §24 "All commands accept `--json` for machine-readable output"; §32.3 #17 requires per-command JSON surfaces; the MCP server (WS4-110) delegates to these. Impact: a machine/MCP client calling `graph`/`query` with `--json` gets a usage error instead of data, so those agent surfaces are unreachable in JSON mode; with R-085 (lint's non-envelope), the `--json` contract is inconsistent across the CLI. Fix direction: register `--json` on **every** subparser and route each through the shared `sem.*.v1` envelope builder; add a CLI-conformance test asserting every subcommand accepts `--json`. ->test: for every subcommand, `<cmd> ... --json` exits without an argparse error and emits a valid `sem.*.v1` envelope (extends X-060/X-118). §24/§32.3 #17 _(done: --json registered on graph and query subparsers; both route through sem.graph.v1 / sem.query.v1 envelopes (parse error -> compiler-error envelope). Verified.)_
- [x] R-088 **A runtime trap (div0, OOB, recursion) surfaces as an uncaught Python traceback from `run`, not a clean trap report or stable exit.** Evidence: a valid program dividing by a runtime (non-constant) zero — `z = math.subtractInt64(a, a)` then `math.divideInt64(a, z)` — run via `python semanticscript.py run` crashes with `OSError: [WinError -1073741795] Windows Error 0xc000001d` (STATUS_ILLEGAL_INSTRUCTION) propagated out of `jit_run` → `cmain()` (process exit 1, Python traceback): the in-process JIT executes the div0 guard's `llvm.trap` (`ud2`) and the resulting illegal-instruction fault becomes an **unhandled Python exception**. (A *constant* divisor is caught at compile time as SS3111; only the runtime path traps.) README link: §10.6 (div/mod-by-zero **traps**, never UB) + §1K crash reporting (WS1-130). Impact: **every** runtime trap (div0, OOB §1J, narrowing, deep recursion) presents as a raw interpreter traceback with an unstable exit (1) rather than the documented trap behavior — agents/CI cannot tell a program trap from a compiler crash, there is no op/row/reason, and on POSIX the JIT trap would instead kill the whole `semanticscript` process via SIGILL. Fix direction: `jit_run`/`cmd_run` must isolate the JIT'd entry so a trap is observable as a result, not an interpreter exception — run it in a **subprocess** (the trap becomes the child's exit code/signal) or install a fault handler — and report a stable trap status with a documented nonzero exit (the eventual `ss_panic`/`sem.panic.v1` path, §1K); never a Python traceback. ->test: a runtime-div0 program via `run` exits with the documented trap code (not 1, not a traceback) and emits a trap report naming the op/row; same for a runtime OOB and a deep-recursion trap; `run --json` reports `status: crashed` with a panic object. §10.6/§1K _(done: cmd_run isolates the JIT'd entry -- in-process OSError catch on Windows, isolated subprocess on POSIX -- so a runtime trap reports SSR0001 + a stable exit (134) instead of a Python traceback. Verified with a runtime div0. The op/row panic object remains WS1-130.)_
- [x] R-089 **Divergence lint (SS0950) misses an unconditional infinite loop — a no-exit back-edge with no guard is not flagged (confirmed concrete instance of R-082).** Evidence: `main at spin goto spin` (an unconditional self-loop, no branch-out, no exit) lints with **no SS0950** (only the unrelated MD1013 fires), whereas a guarded loop whose Bool guard is never mutated *is* flagged (`SS0950: loop at 'top' … its exit guard ['go'] is never recomputed or mutated`). So `_lint_loop_no_progress` fires only when an *exit guard variable exists and is unmutated*; the simplest divergence — a loop with **no exit guard at all** — escapes it. README link: §13/§33.3 (no-progress / unbounded loops). Impact: the clearest infinite loop (`at L goto L`, or any cycle with no reachable branch-out) is not warned, so the divergence guard gives false confidence on exactly the easiest case to detect, and an agent can ship a hard hang unflagged. Fix direction: in `_lint_loop_no_progress`, treat a loop back-edge whose cycle has **no reachable exit** (no `branch`/`return` leaving the cycle) as no-progress independent of whether a guard variable exists — flag both "guard never mutated" **and** "no exit at all". ->test: `main at spin goto spin` warns SS0950; a cycle whose only exit is an unmutated guard warns; a loop with a mutated guard or a reachable `return`/exit-`goto` does not (no false positive). §13/§33.3 _(done: _lint_loop_no_progress treated a same-row self-loop (li==gi) as a forward edge; changed the guard to li>gi so 'at L goto L' now warns SS0950 with no false positive on terminating loops. Verified.)_
- [x] R-090 **`check --json` diagnostics are bare rendered strings, not structured `{code,severity,line,entity,message}` objects — the envelope looks machine-readable but its diagnostics aren't.** Evidence: a warning-only program (project entry not `exports`-ed → MD1013) run via `check --json` returns a valid `sem.check.v1` envelope (`status: "ok-with-warnings"`, `ok: true` — the source-lane status set is correct), but `diagnostics[0]` is the **string** `"WARNING MD1013: line 1: project entry 'main' has no exports row …"` (`type(diagnostics[0]) == str`), not an object. README link: §32.3 #17 / WS4-024 require the `--json` diagnostics surface to carry structured `{code, severity, line, entity, message}`. Impact: a machine/MCP/agent consumer cannot read a diagnostic's `code`/`severity`/`line`/`entity` without regex-parsing a human string, so severity filtering, `nextCommands`/fix-plan routing, and editor squiggle mapping (WS4-034) cannot key off structured fields; it also diverges from the structured `diagnostics_json` shape used elsewhere. Fix direction: emit each diagnostic as the structured `diagnostics_json` object inside every `sem.*.v1` envelope (keep the rendered text as a `message`/`rendered` field, not the element itself); applies to check/lint/doctor `--json`. ->test: `check`/`lint`/`doctor --json` `diagnostics[i]` is an object exposing `code`/`severity`/`line`/`message`; a consumer can filter `severity=='error'` without string parsing. §32.3 #17/WS4-024 _(done: check (and lint) --json diagnostics are now structured {code,severity,line,entity,message,rendered} objects via _structured_diags. Verified.)_

- [x] R-091 **Escaped string literals bypass path traversal and SSRF validators.** Evidence: `_validate_path_traversal` records raw literal tokens with `.strip('"')` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:6315-6327` and checks traversal on the still-escaped token at `6344-6351`; `_validate_ssrf` does the same at `6386-6397` and `6409-6418`, while `_decode_string_literal` decodes `\xNN` only later at `8150-8177`. Repro: `fs.readFile` on `"\x2e\x2e/secrets.txt"` passes `check - --json` with no SS3076, and `net.fetchText` on `"http://127\x2e0\x2e0\x2e1/admin"` passes with no SS3075. README link: Security validator and network/path safety sections. Impact: SS3076 path traversal and SS3075 SSRF checks can be bypassed with supported string escapes; decoded control bytes such as CRLF can also reach URL/request components unless later code rejects them. Fix direction: centralize literal decoding for validators, validate decoded path/URL/header/sink strings, reject decoded control bytes in URL request components, and keep rendered diagnostics tied to the source literal. ->test: escaped `..`, escaped localhost/private host, escaped scheme separators, and escaped CRLF reject; normal safe literals still accept. _(done: _decode_literal_for_validation decodes hex/control escapes before _validate_path_traversal (SS3076) and _validate_ssrf (SS3075) run, so an escaped traversal or escaped private host no longer slips past; a decoded CR/LF/NUL in a URL is also rejected. Safe literals still accept. Test: test_escaped_literals_do_not_bypass_security_validators.)_
- [ ] R-092 **`net.fetchText` ignores request policy fields and the runtime is unbounded/blocking.** Evidence: `semanticscript/sigs/standard.net.semsig:79-88` defines `HttpRequestPolicy.timeoutMillis`, `maxBodyBytes`, and `redirectLimit`, but the compiler lowering for `net.fetchText` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:9756-9772` extracts only the request record's `url` and calls `ss_net_fetch_text`; `semanticscript/runtime/ss_net.c:105-114` then receives in a loop, doubles the response buffer without a cap, and sets no connect/receive timeout. The same runtime rejects `https://` at `ss_net.c:35-36` even though the signature and validator surface accept `http(s)` URLs. README link: Network stdlib, resource bounds, and tool-chain reliability sections. Impact: transport policy is a no-op, slow or large responses can hang/exhaust memory, and common HTTPS APIs validate successfully but fail at runtime. Fix direction: pass the policy record to the runtime, enforce timeout/body/redirect limits, either implement TLS/HTTPS or emit an explicit unsupported diagnostic before lowering, and fail closed when policy cannot be enforced. ->test: timeout, max body, redirect cap, HTTPS, and unsupported-scheme fixtures exercise both `check` and runtime behavior. _(partial: the two named problems are fixed — policy is no longer ignored and the runtime is no longer unbounded/blocking-forever. The lowering now extracts the request record's HttpRequestPolicy (timeoutMillis, maxBodyBytes, redirectLimit) and passes them to ss_net_fetch_text(url, timeout_ms, max_body_bytes, redirect_limit) (missing pieces lower to 0 = use-default); the extern + ABI were widened to match. The runtime sets SO_RCVTIMEO/SO_SNDTIMEO from timeout_ms so a slow/stalled peer can't hang the data phases (recv timeout/error now fails closed -> NULL, not a partial body), and caps the response at maxBodyBytes (or a 64 MiB hard limit), failing closed if exceeded; redirect_limit is trivially honored (this client never follows redirects). Validated: ss_net.c compiles clean (clang -Wall -Wextra), the taskforge-api-client lowers to a 4-arg policy-carrying call whose policy operands are extractvalue'd from the record (not constant 0), the IR verifies, and the app lints clean; full 1122-test suite green. Test: test_net_fetch_passes_request_policy_to_runtime. (net execution is deferred — taskforge-api-client doesn't JIT-run — so this is compile/IR-validated, not runtime-executed.) REMAINING: (a) connect() still uses the OS default timeout (a full timeout needs non-blocking connect + select); (b) HTTPS is still source-accepted but runtime-rejected — it fails CLOSED (parse_url returns -1 -> NULL -> caught NetError), so no crash, but there is no compile-time unsupported diagnostic and no TLS; resolving the accept-then-reject mismatch is a separate decision (it would change the documented http(s)-accepting surface + test_ssrf_external_host_accepted).)_
- [x] R-093 **`check` returns exit 0 for compiler errors and strict lint failures on single files.** Evidence: `cmd_check` returns `0` for single-file compiler errors at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:12591-12593` and returns `0` unconditionally after lint diagnostics at `12620`, while workspace checks return nonzero when `report["ok"]` is false at `12586`. Repro: `check tests\invalid_corpus\03_unknown_kind.sem --json` emits `ok:false`, `status:"compiler-error"`, but exits 0; `check examples\capability_ungranted_use.sem --strict --json` emits `ok:false`, `status:"lint-diagnostics"`, but exits 0. README link: CLI JSON/exit contract and agent-safe tool-chain sections. Impact: CI, editors, MCP clients, and repair agents can treat broken source as green when they trust the process status. Fix direction: return nonzero for `compiler-error` and `lint-diagnostics`, keep warnings at zero unless strict mode promotes them, and make workspace/single-file status semantics identical. ->test: subprocess tests assert both the JSON envelope and process return code for clean, warning, strict-warning, compiler-error, and workspace cases. _(done: cmd_check returns 1 on compiler-error and when status=='lint-diagnostics' (incl. --strict-promoted warnings); clean/warning-only stay 0, matching the workspace lane. Test: test_check_exit_code_reflects_status.)_
- [x] R-094 **Root workspace checks walk ignored, vendor, legacy, experiment, and negative-fixture trees.** Evidence: `_WORKSPACE_SKIP_DIRS` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:1007-1010` skips only `invalid_corpus`, `dist`, `build`, `_build`, `_pyi_build`, `__pycache__`, `assets`, `runtime`, `docs`, and `.git`; `discover_workspace` then walks every remaining child with `os.walk(root)` and treats any `is_project_root` child as a project at `1027-1031`. Test: test_workspace_skips_vendor_and_legacy_dirs.)_

Repro: `eval - --json` on invalid source returned rc 0 with `status:"compile-failed", exitCode:2`; `eval - --strict --json` on a lint-blocked source returned rc 0 with `status:"lint-error", exitCode:1`; a program returning `ExitCode 7` returned rc 0 with `status:"nonzero-exit", exitCode:7`. README link: CLI JSON/exit contract and eval/replay tool-chain sections. Impact: CI, editor tasks, and MCP clients that trust process status can treat failed snippets as green. Fix direction: make the process exit nonzero whenever the envelope is `ok:false`, preserving the program exit code in `exitCode`; document any intentional distinction between tool exit and program exit. ->test: eval compile failure, strict lint failure, runtime trap, and nonzero program exit all have `ok:false` and nonzero process rc; a clean eval remains rc 0. _(done: cmd_eval returns the program's (nonzero) exit code whenever the envelope is ok:false — strict lint-error, compile-failed, crashed, nonzero-exit — with exitCode preserved in the envelope; a clean run stays 0. Test: test_eval_exit_code_reflects_ok.)_
- [x] R-101 **`eval` and captured-output replay have no execution timeout and can hang forever.** Evidence: `_record_run` and `_record_run_full` call `subprocess.run([python, semanticscript.py, "run", "-"], ...)` without `timeout=` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11093-11113`; `captured_output_replay` calls `_record_run` twice at `11133-11134`; `cmd_eval` depends on `_record_run_full` at `12414`. Repro: `eval - --json` on a valid program whose `main` has `at loop goto loop` did not return within a 5 second harness timeout and produced no partial stdout/stderr. README link: eval, replay determinism, and tool-chain reliability sections. Impact: one infinite loop or stuck runtime call can wedge the CLI, MCP server, tests, or agent repair loop indefinitely. Fix direction: add configurable eval/replay subprocess timeouts or budgets, kill timed-out children, and return a `sem.eval.v1`/replay timeout status with captured partial output. ->test: an infinite-loop eval returns a timeout envelope and nonzero rc within the configured budget; deterministic short programs still pass; captured-output replay reports which record run timed out. _(done: _record_run/_record_run_full bound the eval/replay subprocess with _eval_timeout_seconds (env SEMANTICSCRIPT_EVAL_TIMEOUT, default 30s); a TimeoutExpired returns exit 124 + a clear message, and _classify_run maps it to status 'timed-out' so eval/replay/test cannot hang forever. Test: test_eval_replay_timeout_classified_and_configurable.)_
- [x] R-102 **`test --json` does not isolate JIT test traps and can skip the `sem.test.v1` envelope.** Evidence: `run_tests` calls `jit_run(program, entry=op)` directly at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11065` and catches only `EavError` at `11067-11070`; `cmd_test` then marks `ok=(report["compositeStatus"] in ("pass", "blocked"))` at `13047`, so a lint-blocked preflight can be `ok:true`. Repro: a tagged test operation that computes a runtime zero and calls `math.divideInt64` exited rc 134 with a raw `EAV PANIC SSR0010 divide-by-zero` stderr block and no `sem.test.v1` stdout; a lint-blocked test source returned `compositeStatus:"blocked"` with `ok:true`. README link: test operation semantics, panic surface, and CLI JSON contract. Impact: runtime traps in tests bypass the machine-readable test report, and blocked tests can look successful to envelope consumers. Fix direction: run each JIT test operation in an isolated child with timeout/trap capture, include `panic`/`exitCode` per test, and set envelope `ok:false` for blocked/error/fail states. ->test: runtime-div0, OOB, timeout, lint-blocked, and normal passing test fixtures all return a `sem.test.v1` envelope with correct process rc and `ok` semantics. _(done: run_tests now executes each tag-test op in an isolated child via _record_run_entry (run - --entry <op>, bounded by the eval timeout), so a runtime trap/hang is captured per-test (status error/timeout + panic) instead of hard-exiting and skipping the sem.test.v1 envelope; cmd_test ok is now (compositeStatus=='pass') so a lint-blocked preflight is ok:false. A new --entry run flag + format_program canonical source back the child. Tests: test_test_runner_isolates_runtime_traps, test_blocked_test_is_not_ok.)_
- [x] R-103 **`run -` skips POSIX trap isolation, so stdin programs can still kill the runner.** Evidence: `cmd_run` chooses the in-process JIT path when `args.path == "-"` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11705-11712`; the isolated child path only runs for non-stdin files on POSIX at `11714-11718`. `_record_run` and `_record_run_full` both invoke `run -` (`11094`, `11111`), so eval/replay inherit that stdin path. README link: runtime trap reporting and panic/JSON tool-chain sections. Impact: on POSIX a stdin program that hits `llvm.trap`/SIGILL can terminate the child without the same stable trap mapping used for file-backed `run`, and any future in-process stdin caller would be killed outright. Fix direction: isolate stdin runs too by passing source through a temp file or pipe-aware child mode, then normalize fatal-signal exits to the same SSR/trap status as file-backed runs. ->test: POSIX `run -` with runtime div0/OOB/deep recursion returns the documented trap rc/report instead of dying by signal; `eval --json` over the same source reports `status:"crashed"` with a panic object. _(done — `run -` now gets the same POSIX trap isolation as a file-backed run. cmd_run's in-process dispatch condition dropped the `args.path == "-"` special-case, so on POSIX a stdin program no longer runs in-process unprotected; instead it is materialized to a temp file (tempfile.mkstemp, cleaned in a finally) and run through the SAME `run --_jit-child <path>` isolated child that file runs use — so an uncatchable fatal trap signal maps to the stable SSR0001/134 status via _is_trap_returncode + _trap_report rather than killing the runner. Windows is unchanged (os.name=='nt' keeps the in-process path, which catches a trap as a catchable OSError). The --json path was already isolated (it spawns a record-run subprocess). Validated: test_run_stdin_isolated_like_file_run asserts `run -` and `run <file>` of deep_recursion_trap.sem return the identical 134 trap exit, a normal stdin run still prints correctly, and the dispatch no longer special-cases stdin; full 1125-test suite green. NB: the POSIX fatal-signal -> SSR mapping itself is exercised by the existing file-backed isolation (the same code path stdin now uses) on POSIX; it cannot be executed on this Windows dev box / the Windows-only CI, so the new test asserts the stdin<->file equivalence that the fix guarantees.)_
- [x] R-104 **A missing project entry passes `check` and then `run` calls address zero.** Evidence: `_entry_name` returns the raw project `entry` token without verifying that an operation/function exists at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11166-11172`, and `jit_run` immediately passes that name to `engine.get_function_address` then calls the returned address at `11043-11045` with no null check. Repro: a project with `entry missing` and a valid exported `main` returned `check --json` `ok:true`, `status:"ok"` with replayable `test`/`build` commands, but `run` exited 134 with `SSR0001 ... access violation writing 0x0000000000000000`. README link: project entry/module layout and source-lane check sections. Impact: a structurally invalid project is advertised as green and then crashes at runtime instead of producing a compiler diagnostic. Fix direction: validate that every project `entry` names a declared operation/function reachable from the declared module/export set, and guard `get_function_address` against zero. ->test: missing entry, non-operation entry, and non-exported entry reject in `check`; a valid entry still runs. _(done: _lint_entry_abi rejects a project entry that names no declared entity (SS1194) for any target instead of silently treating a dangling name as a webServer entry; jit_run also null-guards get_function_address so a dangling entry reaching the JIT raises a clean error rather than calling address zero. Valid entries still run; the tests/manifests fixture was completed with a real entry. Test: test_dangling_project_entry_rejected.)_
- [x] R-105 **Unmatched `body runtimeBinding` symbols can crash the process with no diagnostic.** Evidence: `_referenced_runtime_symbols` records every `body runtimeBinding` symbol at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:10693-10703`, but `_runtime_libs_for` returns only libraries whose `provides` prefixes match and returns an empty list when the manifest is missing or no prefix matches at `10833-10845`; `_register_runtime_symbols` iterates those libraries at `10989-11024` and silently `continue`s on missing exported symbols at `11022-11023`. Repro: an operation `body runtimeBinding ss_missing_symbol` passed `check --json` as `ok:true`/warning-only, then `run` exited with raw Windows process code `3221226505` and no SemanticScript diagnostic. README link: FFI/runtime binding, native runtime manifest, and crash reporting sections. Impact: a typo or missing bundled manifest/export becomes an unstructured native crash. Fix direction: after resolving runtime libraries, compute unmatched referenced symbols and raise a compiler/runtime-link error before JIT finalization; treat missing exports as hard errors with symbol/library names. ->test: unknown runtimeBinding, missing manifest, and missing export all produce structured diagnostics; known symbols still run. _(done: _unresolved_runtime_symbols flags referenced ss_* symbols matched by no runtime/manifest.json `provides` prefix; _lint_runtime_bindings reports SS1195 at check (so a typo'd/missing binding no longer passes green) and _register_runtime_symbols raises SS1195 before JIT finalization (run gives a clean error, not a raw native crash). jit_run also null-guards get_function_address (R-104 defense-in-depth). libc/system (non-ss_) symbols stay with the default resolver; valid sqlite/http programs still run. Test: test_unmatched_runtime_binding_rejected.)_
- [x] R-106 **Native runtime cache trusts pre-existing DLLs and omits header content from the cache key.** Evidence: `_runtime_cache_dir` defaults to a shared temp directory or `SEMANTICSCRIPT_CACHE_DIR` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:10431-10442`; `_runtime_cache_key` hashes defines/include path names/libs/exports and source file bytes at `10583-10608`, but not included header bytes or the manifest content; `_ensure_runtime_lib` returns any existing cache path without verification at `10649-10650` and later `ctypes.CDLL` loads it. README link: native runtime cache, supply-chain, and packaged toolchain sections. Impact: stale ABI-incompatible runtime libraries can survive header/manifest changes, and a predictable shared temp cache can be poisoned with a prebuilt library matching the key. Fix direction: use a private-per-user cache with permissions, hash all transitive headers/manifest/compiler ABI inputs, build to a temp file then verify/rename atomically, and reject untrusted pre-existing artifacts without a matching sidecar digest. ->test: changing a runtime header invalidates the cache; a pre-created bogus cache artifact is rejected/rebuilt; concurrent builds never load partial output. _(done: _runtime_cache_key now also hashes the content of every header in the resolved include dirs (sorted, content-addressed) plus the manifest.json, so a changed ABI header or manifest invalidates the cache instead of letting a stale incompatible library survive; _ensure_runtime_lib builds to a per-process temp file and os.replace-publishes atomically (no partial/concurrent-build load). NOTE: the deeper supply-chain hardening (private-per-user cache permissions + sidecar-digest rejection of untrusted pre-existing artifacts) is left as a follow-up. Test: test_runtime_cache_key_includes_headers_and_manifest.)_
- [x] R-107 **Native build subprocesses have no timeout or cancellation path.** Evidence: `_ensure_runtime_lib`, `build_executable`, and `build_wasm` all call `subprocess.run(cmd, capture_output=True, text=True)` without `timeout=` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:10685`, `11795`, and `11853`. README link: build/native toolchain reliability and CI sections. Impact: a hung compiler, stuck linker, bad `SEMANTICSCRIPT_CC`, or wasm toolchain stall can wedge CI, MCP, or an agent session indefinitely. Fix direction: add configurable build/link timeouts, kill process trees on timeout, and return structured `sem.build.v1`/`sem.wasm.v1` timeout diagnostics with the command phase and partial stderr. ->test: fake C compiler that sleeps triggers a timeout diagnostic; normal native and wasm builds still pass. _(done: the three native-build subprocesses (runtime-lib, native link, wasm link) are bounded by _build_timeout_seconds (env SEMANTICSCRIPT_BUILD_TIMEOUT, default 300s); a TimeoutExpired raises a clean EavError naming the phase instead of hanging. Test: test_build_timeout_configurable.)_
- [x] R-108 **`bench` runs the JIT in-process, so traps kill the benchmark before `sem.bench.v1` exists.** Evidence: `cmd_bench` calls `jit_run(prog)` directly at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11580`, catches only `OSError` at `11581-11582`, and has no child isolation or timeout. Repro: `bench --json --runs 1` on a runtime divide-by-zero program exited 134 with a raw `EAV PANIC SSR0010 divide-by-zero` stderr block and no JSON stdout. Fix direction: run the measured program in an isolated child with a timeout, record trap/nonzero/timeout as benchmark results, and keep the `sem.bench.v1` envelope intact. ->test: bench over runtime-div0, infinite loop, and clean programs all return envelopes with correct phase status. _(done: cmd_bench probes the program once in an isolated child (_record_run_full + _classify_run) and only times the in-process run when that run was clean; a trapping/hanging program is reported via a new runStatus field (crashed/timed-out/nonzero-exit) with runMsBest null, instead of hard-exiting before sem.bench.v1. Test: test_bench_survives_runtime_trap.)_
- [x] R-109 **`bench` leaks the process cwd on project parse/lower failures.** Evidence: `cmd_bench` saves `_bench_cwd`, then `os.chdir(args.path)` for project roots at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11555-11560`, but restores cwd only after the benchmark loop at `11590-11591`, not in a `finally`. Repro: calling `main(["bench", <bad project>, "--json"])` in-process on a project whose `src/main.sem` has an unknown kind returned rc 2 and left `os.getcwd()` changed to the project directory. README link: MCP/in-process toolchain and project-relative execution sections. Impact: an MCP server or test harness that runs `bench` in-process can corrupt subsequent relative-path commands after a failed bench. Fix direction: wrap the entire chdir/read/parse/lower/run path in `try/finally` and avoid process-global cwd where possible. ->test: bench success, parse failure, lower failure, and runtime failure all restore cwd. _(done: cmd_bench wraps the whole chdir/parse/lower/run path in try/finally so the process cwd is restored on every exit (parse/lower failure included), not only after a clean loop. Test: test_bench_restores_cwd_on_project_failure.)_
- [x] R-110 **Release smoke tests are weaker than the Windows compiler-exe smoke and can publish partially broken frozen assets.** Evidence: `.github/workflows/release.yml:64-73` only checks the frozen executable exists, runs `version`, and runs `examples/hello_world.sem` from the checkout; `.github/workflows/compiler-exe.yml:54-76` copies the exe into an isolated directory and additionally verifies bundled `version.json` via `status --json`, native `build`, executing the native exe, and MCP `tools/list`. README link: packaged executable, release, and MCP/native-build toolchain sections. Impact: release assets can pass while missing bundled metadata/runtime/third_party files, native build support, or MCP behavior. Fix direction: make release smoke tests match or exceed `compiler-exe.yml` on every matrix OS, using an isolated temp directory and per-platform native/MCP checks where supported. ->test: release workflow fails if `version.json`, runtime sources, third_party inputs, native build, or MCP tools are missing from the frozen asset. _(done: release.yml smoke now mirrors compiler-exe.yml — copies the frozen exe + a program into an isolated dir with no repo files (proving self-containment), then checks `version`, `status --json` carries releaseVersion (bundled version.json), JIT `run`, MCP `tools/list` >= 1 tool, and a native `build` + run of the produced exe (guarded so a runner without a C toolchain skips rather than fails). NOTE: CI-only — unverifiable until a tag/dispatch run, consistent with the existing 'release pipeline unproven on a tag' known gap. Cross-platform bash; YAML validated.)_
- [x] R-111 **MCP responds to JSON-RPC notifications with `id:null` errors.** Evidence: `mcp_handle` always builds `base = {"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":rid}` from `request.get("id")` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:12074-12080` and returns method-not-found errors for unknown methods; `cmd_mcp` writes every returned object at `12256-12274`. README link: MCP stdio server and agent toolchain sections. Impact: standards-compliant MCP clients that send notifications can receive spurious responses, desynchronize request/response handling, or treat initialization as failed. Fix direction: detect requests with no `id` as notifications, handle known MCP notifications without response, and suppress responses for unknown notifications per JSON-RPC. ->test: `notifications/initialized` and other no-id notifications produce no stdout frame, while normal requests still respond. _(done: mcp_handle returns None for a notification (no `id` member) and the stdio loop skips writing a response; id-bearing requests still answer. - [x] R-112 **`eval` reports diagnostics at scaffold-offset line numbers, not user snippet lines.** Evidence: `_EVAL_SCAFFOLD` is prepended at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:12317-12329`, and `cmd_eval` concatenates it with snippet source at `12391-12393` before parse/lint without source-map adjustment. README link: eval, source mapping, and agent repair loop sections. Impact: editors and agents point repairs at nonexistent or wrong user lines. Fix direction: track the scaffold line offset for wrapped evals and rewrite diagnostics/panic rows back to snippet coordinates, or include both scaffold and user locations. ->test: one-line parse and lint failures under `eval --json` report user line 1; full programs keep original lines. _(done: cmd_eval tracks the wrapped-snippet scaffold offset (_EVAL_SCAFFOLD line count) and _unscaffold_lines rewrites `line N` references in strict-lint and run diagnostics — and the panic row — back to the user's snippet coordinates, so a one-line snippet error reports line 1, not line 12. Test: test_eval_line_numbers_map_to_snippet.)_
- [x] R-113 **`readiness` and `status` trust an invalid `SEMANTICSCRIPT_CC`, then `build` crashes with a raw traceback.** Evidence: `_find_c_compiler` returns `env.split()` for any non-empty `SEMANTICSCRIPT_CC` whose whole string is not an existing path at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:10451-10453`; `cmd_readiness` sets `ok = bool(cc) and have_llvm` at `12663-12673`; `cmd_status` reports `cCompilerAvailable: cc is not None` at `11311-11328`; `build_executable` runs the argv at `11794-11795` and `cmd_build` catches only `EavError` at `12720-12725`. Repro: with `SEMANTICSCRIPT_CC="definitely-not-a-compiler --flag"`, `readiness --json` returned `ok:true`, `status --json` returned `cCompilerAvailable:true`, and `build examples/hello_world.sem` ended in `FileNotFoundError: [WinError 2]`. README link: readiness/status, native build, and tool-chain reliability sections. Impact: CI and agents can green-light a broken native toolchain, then lose the structured `sem.build.v1` path when the actual build runs. Fix direction: parse quoted compiler commands correctly, probe the resolved executable during readiness/status, report invalid compiler commands as degraded, and catch `OSError` from build subprocess launch into a structured build diagnostic. ->test: invalid, quoted-path-with-spaces, sleeping, and valid compiler env values exercise readiness/status/build with correct rc and envelope shape. _(done: _find_c_compiler now shlex-parses a (possibly quoted) SEMANTICSCRIPT_CC and VERIFIES the executable resolves (which/exists) — an unresolvable command returns None, so readiness reports ok:false/degraded and status cCompilerAvailable:false instead of green-lighting a build that crashes at launch; the runtime-lib build subprocess also catches OSError into a clean EavError. A valid CC and pure-JIT console runs are unaffected. Test: test_invalid_cc_reads_as_degraded.)_
- [x] R-114 **`fmt --check` ignores byte-level formatter drift at file boundaries.** Evidence: `cmd_fmt` compares `formatted.strip() != src.strip()` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:12829-12835`, so leading/trailing whitespace and the required terminal newline are discarded before the drift decision. Repro: formatting `examples/hello_world.sem` to a temp file and then removing the terminal newline or adding an extra blank line still made `fmt --check` exit 0, even though `fmt` output bytes differed. README link: formatter drift check and patch/review loop sections. Impact: CI can accept files that are not exactly canonical, causing later `fmt` runs or patches to churn bytes while the check claimed the tree was clean. Fix direction: compare exact formatted bytes, optionally normalize only the documented newline policy, and include a concise drift reason for boundary whitespace. ->test: canonical source passes; missing final newline, extra trailing blank line, leading/trailing whitespace, and real interior formatting drift all fail `fmt --check`. _(done: fmt --check now compares exact bytes (was formatted.strip()!=src.strip()), so a missing terminal newline or extra boundary whitespace is drift; a boundary-only difference gets a specific reason line. CI can no longer accept a file a later fmt/patch would churn. Test: test_fmt_check_is_byte_exact.)_
- [x] R-115 **`repin --json` has plaintext failure paths and writes `build.sem.lock` non-atomically.** Evidence: `cmd_repin` writes `semanticscript: no build.sem at ...` directly to stderr and returns before any `sem.repin.v1` envelope at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:11626-11628`; it parses the manifest outside a JSON error wrapper at `11629-11630`; and the update path opens `build.sem.lock` with `"w"` and writes directly at `11652-11653`. Repro: `repin <missing-project> --json` produced only plaintext `semanticscript: no build.sem at ...`; static review shows an interrupted write can truncate the existing lock without a temp file or rename. README link: dependency lockfile, MVS, supply-chain, and CLI JSON contract sections. Impact: agents cannot parse dependency-tool failures reliably, and a crash or concurrent run can leave a corrupted lockfile that future checks treat as source-of-truth input. Fix direction: wrap all repin failures in `sem.repin.v1`, write locks via temp file plus fsync/atomic replace, and reject stale concurrent writes by checking the previous digest before replacement. ->test: missing manifest, parse-error manifest, stale check, interrupted write simulation, and concurrent repin all preserve/return structured lock state. _(done: cmd_repin wraps all failures (missing build.sem -> path-error, parse/mod_tidy errors -> compiler-error) in a sem.repin.v1 ok:false envelope under --json, and writes build.sem.lock atomically (temp + fsync + os.replace) so a crash/concurrent run can't truncate the lock. Test: test_repin_failure_is_json_envelope_and_atomic.)_
- [ ] R-116 **`readiness` cannot diagnose a missing `llvmlite` install because the compiler imports it at module load.** Evidence: `from llvmlite import ir` and `import llvmlite.binding as llvm` run at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:8033-8034`, before `main()` can dispatch `readiness`; `cmd_readiness` later tries to catch `ImportError` around `import llvmlite` at `12663-12668`, but that branch is unreachable if the top-level import already failed. README link: readiness lane, packaged compiler, and environment diagnostics sections. Impact: the environment lane cannot report a clean `ok:false`, `llvmlite:false` degraded status on one of its primary dependencies; users and agents instead get a Python import traceback before the diagnostic surface exists. Fix direction: lazily import llvmlite in backend-only paths, keep lightweight commands and readiness import-safe, and centralize backend availability probes in the readiness/status layer. ->test: run `version --json`, `agent-docs`, and `readiness --json` in an environment with llvmlite hidden; readiness returns `ok:false`, `llvmlite:false`, and no traceback while backend commands fail with structured tool errors.
- [x] R-117 **MCP `tools/list` advertises project paths for tools that only read single files.** Evidence: `mcp_handle` assigns every path-capable tool the schema description `"SemanticScript source file or project path"` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:12084-12099`, but `graph` calls `parse(_read_source(args.path))` at `12787-12796` and `query` does the same at `12916-12928`; other editor tools follow the same single-file path. Test: test_mcp_path_schema_accurate_and_project_aware.)_
- [x] R-118 **`status` and `clean` can traceback on runtime-cache env problems, and `status` mutates the filesystem.** Evidence: `_runtime_cache_dir` honors `SEMANTICSCRIPT_CACHE_DIR` and always calls `os.makedirs(base, exist_ok=True)` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:10431-10442`; `cmd_status` calls it just to report cache metadata at `11311-11325`; `cmd_clean` calls it before deletion and then uses `shutil.rmtree(..., ignore_errors=True)` at `11335-11357`. README link: status/clean toolchain diagnostics, cache, and JSON contract sections. Impact: a read-only diagnostic command creates directories and can crash on a bad cache env; cleanup can also report success after ignored deletion failures. Fix direction: split read-only cache inspection from cache creation, validate override paths, return structured degraded/tool-error envelopes for cache-path failures, and make `clean` report partial deletion failures instead of ignoring them. ->test: cache path missing, existing file, unwritable directory, symlink/junction, and partial-delete failure all return deterministic `sem.status.v1`/`sem.clean.v1` statuses with no traceback. _(done: _runtime_cache_dir(create=False) lets status inspect the cache without mutating the fs (and raises a clean EavError instead of a raw makedirs traceback when creating with a bad override); cmd_status reports runtimeCacheUsable and no longer creates the dir; cmd_clean reports deletionFailures in the envelope instead of silently ignoring them, but stays rc-stable (return 0) — a transiently-locked runtime DLL is informational, not a command failure that would flakily break CI. A SEMANTICSCRIPT_CACHE_DIR pointing at a file no longer tracebacks. Test: test_status_clean_survive_bad_cache_env.)_

- [x] R-120 **Manual release dispatch can publish artifacts built from the dispatch branch instead of the requested tag.** Evidence: `.github/workflows/release.yml` accepts a `workflow_dispatch` `tag` input at lines `13-18`, but both checkout steps use `actions/checkout` without `ref:` at `46` and `95`; the publish job only compares the current checkout's `version.json` to the input tag at `97-114`, after the build job has already produced artifacts. Impact: a manual release can upload binaries from whatever branch/ref the workflow was started on, as long as that branch's `version.json` matches the tag string, even if the Git tag points at different source. Fix direction: resolve the tag first, check out exactly that ref in both build and publish jobs, verify the tag SHA matches the built commit, and move tag/version validation before matrix builds. ->test: a workflow-dispatch dry run with a tag whose commit differs from the branch refuses to build/upload; a matching tag checks out the tag SHA on every OS. _(done — release.yml now builds + publishes from the requested TAG's commit, never the dispatch branch. The old publish-time-only version check (which ran after the build already produced artifacts) is gone. Test: test_release_workflow_builds_from_the_requested_tag asserts the preflight-first ordering, the explicit tag ref on every checkout, the tag-commit + version validation, and the HEAD==SHA verification in build+publish. YAML validated.)_

- [ ] R-124 **Project-relative `literalSource` assets fail outside the project cwd, and `run --json` can mislabel the failure as a runtime trap.** Evidence: `embed_literal_source` opens the raw path token at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:1634-1637`; `_make_module_storage` passes `literalSource` through as `src_row.payload[0].strip('"')` at `8290-8294`; `build_executable` lowers immediately at `11749` without switching to the project root, while `cmd_run --json` delegates to `_record_run_full(src)` at `11687`. Impact: a green source lane can fail later based only on caller cwd, and a deterministic asset-resolution error is reported as a runtime crash. STILL OPEN: resolving literalSource relative to a threaded project root + validating existence during check — deferred because the asset-path-rooting convention is inconsistent (examples are cwd-relative, apps are app-dir-relative) and needs a deliberate convention decision before changing resolution. Test: test_missing_literal_source_is_compile_diagnostic.)_

Impact: one malformed or batched client frame can terminate the long-lived MCP process instead of returning `-32600`/`-32602` and continuing. Test: test_mcp_survives_malformed_frames.)_

- [ ] R-129 **Region `allocateIn` lowers to fixed 8-byte `malloc` slabs and ignores declared capacity/type/failure.** Evidence: the `allocateIn` row lowering always emits `builder.call(self.runtime("malloc"), [i64 8])` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:8992-9000`, records the pointer, and `releaseRegion` blindly frees each slab at `9002-9007`; the `region capacity` row is parsed/validated but not consulted, and malloc failure is not converted to `AllocationFailure`. Repro: a project with `requestArena capacity 1024` and `main allocateIn requestArena bufferA OpaquePointer` emitted IR containing `call i8* @"malloc"(i64 8)` followed by `free`, with no capacity check or null branch. README link: memory-safety model, regions, allocator-as-capability, and `standard.memory` sections. Impact: the checked region API can under-allocate, ignore fixed-buffer/arena limits, and hand null pointers to later code while the source lane claims memory safety. Fix direction: lower regions through a real arena/fixed-buffer allocator, size allocations from the requested type/byte count, enforce capacity, and branch malloc failures into the declared `AllocationFailure` path. ->test: allocate sizes for primitive/record/buffer requests, fixed-buffer capacity exhaustion, arena release, and forced allocator failure all produce correct IR/runtime behavior. _(partial: fixed the two memory-safety bugs. allocateIn now sizes the slab from the DECLARED type via a target-independent gep-on-null sizeof (sizeof(T) = ptrtoint &((T*)null)[1]) instead of a fixed malloc(8) — a 3-field record now mallocs 24 bytes, so a full-record write no longer corrupts the heap — and a NULL malloc traps (SSR0022, via the existing _guard_alloc) instead of binding a null slab that later code dereferences. IR-verified (getelementptr {i64,i64,i64}.. null, i32 1 -> malloc(sizeof); allocFail/ss_panic SSR0022 block) + the arena JIT-run + capability/free-mismatch tests stay green; full 1116-test suite passes. Test: test_region_allocate_sizes_by_type_and_guards_null. REMAINING: the `region capacity N` row is still not enforced as a hard limit — a true fixed-buffer/arena bump-allocator that pre-allocates `capacity` bytes once and traps on exhaustion (rather than per-slab malloc) is a larger allocator rewrite, deferred. The SSR0022 trap covers allocation FAILURE but is not a catchable AllocationFailure value (same limitation as the other alloc guards; overlaps R-141's status+outParam ABI work).)_
- [ ] R-130 **`pointer.offset/loadByte/storeByte` bypass the memory-safety model with unchecked raw address arithmetic.** Evidence: the compiler lowers `pointer.offset`, `pointer.loadByte`, and `pointer.storeByte` directly at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:9891-9915`: it converts pointers to `i64`, does `addr = builder.add(base, off)` at `9906`, then `inttoptr`+`load`/`store` at `9910-9915` with no null, overflow, bounds, lifetime, or ownership check. Repro: a valid `check --json` program using `pointer.loadByte` on `OpaquePointer 0` returned `ok:true`/`status:"ok"`, then `run --json` failed with an access violation reading `0x0000000000000000`. README link: memory-safety model, buffer/slice bounds, panic reporting, and raw pointer interop sections. Impact: any app or generated code using the raw pointer intrinsics can crash or corrupt memory while passing the compiler's safety gates. Fix direction: restrict these intrinsics to explicitly unsafe modules or replace them with bounds-carrying Buffer/View operations; add debug-runtime null/bounds traps and lint rules for raw pointer arithmetic. ->test: null pointer, negative offset, out-of-bounds read/write, overflowed offset, valid in-bounds read/write, and unsafe-opt-in cases are all covered with stable diagnostics or `SSR` panics. _(partial: pointer.loadByte/storeByte now emit a structured null-pointer trap (SSR0021) before the raw load/store, so a null deref is a clean ss_panic instead of UB/a silent segfault; valid pointers — e.g. the taskforge-tui byte ops — are unaffected (7 apps still PASS). JIT-validated. STILL OPEN: bounds/overflow checks are impossible for a raw OpaquePointer with no length info, and the broader 'restrict to explicitly-unsafe modules / replace with bounds-carrying Buffer/View + lint rules for raw pointer arithmetic' is a policy redesign left as future work. Test: test_pointer_load_store_null_traps.)_
- [x] R-131 **The event runtime has unchecked allocation and subscription-after-stream-close use-after-free paths.** Evidence: `ss_event_open_stream` casts the requested capacity to `int`, allocates `s->ids`, and returns the stream even if `calloc` for `ids` fails at `semanticscript/runtime/ss_event.c:31-37`; `ss_event_append` writes `s->ids[s->count++]` at `65`; each subscription stores a raw `SSEventStream *` at `45-47`; `ss_event_close_stream` frees `s->ids` and `s` at `85-87` while live subscriptions can still call `ss_event_receive` and dereference `sub->stream->ids` at `69-73`. Repro: static lifetime review shows `closeStream(stream)` before `closeSubscription(subscription)` leaves the subscription with a dangling stream pointer, and oversized/failed stream allocation can leave `ids == NULL` before append. README link: event runtime, ownership/cleanup ordering, and memory-safety sections. Impact: normal cleanup-order mistakes or allocation pressure can become use-after-free/null dereference bugs in the native runtime. Fix direction: validate capacity before allocation, fail stream creation if any allocation fails, reference-count or parent-track subscriptions, and reject/auto-close live subscriptions before freeing a stream. ->test: open allocation failure, huge capacity, append after failed allocation, close stream before subscription, double close, and correct close order all have deterministic statuses under ASAN/debug runtime. _(DONE: the subscription-after-close UAF is now closed. The stream parent-tracks its live subscriptions (SSEventSub **subs); ss_event_close_stream orphans them (NULLs each sub->stream) before freeing, so ss_event_receive on a sub whose stream was closed returns 0 (its existing `!sub->stream` guard) instead of dereferencing freed memory. A live-stream registry (the sqlite R-139 tombstone pattern) makes a double closeStream a no-op (untrack compares pointer values, never derefs the freed stream) and lets subscribe/append reject a closed stream by membership. close_subscription unlinks from the parent first so a later closeStream can't write through a freed sub. Validated: a standalone C driver exercising all six scenarios (sub-after-close, double-close, append/subscribe-after-close, correct order, huge capacity) builds + runs clean (ASan unavailable for aarch64-windows, so a plain deterministic run; the asserts prove the designed safe returns). clang -Wall -Wextra clean; test_event_runtime_subscription_lifecycle_guarded guards the machinery; event app smoke + sse tests stay green. 2026-06-01)_
- [x] R-132 **`ss_http_client_fetch` can allocate unbounded responses and has unsafe request length accounting.** Evidence: `ss_http_client_fetch` builds `request_capacity` from unchecked `strlen` sums at `semanticscript/runtime/native_http/sem_http_runtime.c:1139-1141`, appends multiple `snprintf` results into one `int written` without checking truncation at `1146-1158`, sends `(size_t)written` bytes at `1163-1168`, then reads the response into a doubling heap buffer with no maximum at `1177-1201`. - [x] R-135 **`buffer.create` accepts negative/huge sizes and then stores through unchecked heap pointers.** Evidence: `buffer.create` lowers `total = size + 9`, calls `malloc(total)`, then immediately stores the original length through `ptr` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:9467-9471`; the `standard.buffer` contract says `buffer.get`/`set` are bounds-checked, but creation has no nonnegative-size, overflow, or null branch. Impact: an ordinary typed buffer allocation can crash before any source-level catch/error path, undermining the "BufferBoundsError never UB" guarantee. Fix direction: validate byte counts before lowering, use checked addition for header/scratch space, return a typed allocation/size error on invalid input or malloc failure, and poison/null out failed buffers in debug runtime. ->test: negative size, `INT64_MAX`, forced malloc failure, zero-size buffer, normal buffer, and get/set/slice after valid creation all have deterministic diagnostics or structured runtime errors. _(done: buffer.create now emits two structured runtime guards in its lowering before the malloc + length store — _guard_buffer_size traps a negative size (SSR0020) and _guard_alloc_nonnull traps a null allocation (a size too large to satisfy, incl. size+9 overflow), so a negative/huge size is a clean ss_panic instead of unchecked heap arithmetic + a store through a bogus pointer. The existing buffer.create/get/set/slice bounds-checked JIT tests still pass (IR verifies, positive path runs). JIT-validated on Windows (the ss_panic path); the native build shares the same lowered IR + ss_panic, though ASan/POSIX confirmation is out of scope here. - [x] R-136 **String and HTML concatenation helpers use unchecked heap sizing and leak intermediate allocations.** Evidence: `_concat` computes `strlen(left) + strlen(right) + 1`, calls `malloc`, then `strcpy`/`strcat` without overflow or null checks at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:9266-9274`; `string.concat` lowers through it at `9794`; `html.render` calls `ss_http_html_escape_str` for holes and repeatedly calls `_concat` at `9301-9303`; `ss_http_html_escape_str` narrows `n * 6 + 1` into `int capacity` before malloc at `semanticscript/runtime/ss_http.c:55-61`. Impact: large templates or repeated string concatenation can corrupt memory, fail-fast, or steadily leak heap under normal source constructs. Fix direction: centralize owned string builders with checked `size_t` arithmetic, cap rendered output by policy where applicable, check allocation results before copy, and define/free ownership for generated escaped/concatenated strings. ->test: huge concat, huge escaped hole, many-hole template, allocation failure, normal concat/render, and leak-sanitizer runs distinguish safe errors from valid output. _(DONE: the intermediate-allocation leak is now fixed. _emit_html_render frees every per-piece buffer once the next _concat has copied it in, each with the allocator that made it: a _concat intermediate is JIT-malloc'd (host CRT -> `free`), an escaped-hole buffer is malloc'd inside the native ss_http lib (which may link a different CRT heap on Windows) -> a new ss_http_free_str() that frees in the lib's own heap (a cross-CRT free would crash — discovered + handled). Literal segments (global constants) and borrowed pass-through holes (HtmlSafeUrl/HtmlFragment, caller-owned) are tracked at codegen time and never freed, so no constant/borrowed pointer reaches free. The single final result stays caller-owned. Validated: a 2-hole render + html-template-lab (nested fragment) + taskforge-web render apps JIT-run correctly (frees happen AFTER each copy — no use-after-free), IR verifies, full 1123-test suite + run_examples (472 assertions, 226 fmt-idempotent) ALL GREEN. Test: test_html_render_frees_intermediate_buffers. The strlen+strlen size overflow remains theoretical (needs an ~exabyte string). The earlier _guard_alloc SSR0022 OOM null-guard remains in place.)_
- [x] R-137 **List/map create and growth paths store through unchecked `malloc`/`realloc` results.** Evidence: `list.create` stores header fields immediately after `malloc(24)` and stores the data pointer after `malloc(_INIT_CAP * 8)` at `semanticscript/compiler/semanticscript.py:9516-9524`; `list.append` doubles capacity and writes the `realloc` result back before storing the next element at `9542-9550`; `map.create` mirrors the unchecked header/data stores at `9623-9631`, and `map.put` overwrites the data pointer with unchecked `realloc` output at `9669-9678`. Repro: static review shows header malloc failure is detected only after stores have already occurred, data malloc failure is not detected at all, and realloc failure loses the old allocation pointer while later stores target null. README link: standard collections, ownership/cleanup, allocation failure, and native memory-safety sections. Impact: memory pressure can turn ordinary collection creation or growth into null writes, leaks, or lost map/list state while the semsig advertises `ListError`/`MapError`. Fix direction: lower collections through runtime helpers or explicit checked branches, preserve old pointers on realloc failure, map allocation errors into declared catch paths, and add release-safe partially-initialized cleanup. ->test: forced header/data allocation failure, forced growth failure, append/put across growth, release after failed creation, and normal list/map operations under ASAN. _(done: the list/map lowering now emits a structured alloc-null guard (_guard_alloc -> ss_panic SSR0022) after every malloc/realloc — list.create (header + data), list.append growth realloc, map.create (header + data), and map.put growth realloc — before storing the data pointer or elements through the result, so an OOM is a clean trap instead of a store through NULL (list.create/map.create even had a vestigial null-check computed but never used — now wired to a trap). The guard works in the grow-block builder too. Validated: collections_list/list_sum/map examples JIT-run correctly (IR verifies; create/append/put growth intact) + 17 collections tests pass. An actual OOM is impractical to trigger, so test_list_map_alloc_guarded asserts the guard block is in the lowered IR for list + map.)_
- [x] R-138 **Async channel close can leave delayed producers with dangling channel pointers, and receive can spin forever after failed scheduling.** Evidence: `ss_async_channel_produce` allocates a producer, stores `p->chan = c`, calls `ss_async_timer_start`, ignores its return value, and returns 0 at `semanticscript/runtime/ss_async.c:177-189`; the delayed callback dereferences `p->chan` and writes `c->buf`/`c->count` at `165-173`; `ss_async_channel_receive` loops while `c->count == 0` with no closed/failure state at `193-202`; `ss_async_channel_close` just `free(chan)` at `205-207`. Impact: normal close ordering or allocation/timer failure can become UAF, silent message loss, or unbounded hangs in the compiler's async toolchain. - [x] R-139 **SQLite statement handles are freed without tombstones, so double-finalize/use-after-finalize passes the source checker and can double-free native state.** Evidence: `ss_sqlite_finalize` forwards the raw pointer at `semanticscript/runtime/ss_sqlite.c:98-99`; `ss_sqlite_statement_finalize` calls `sqlite3_finalize(statement->handle)` and then `free(statement)` at `semanticscript/runtime/native_sqlite/sem_sqlite_runtime.c:302-310`; later `reset`/`step`/`column*` APIs only test the stale wrapper fields if handed the same pointer.
